Jennifer Garner Details "Incredible" Chapter in Ben Affleck Marriage

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Jennifer Garner pulled back the curtain on a sweet chapter during her marriage to Ben Affleck when she took the pair’s three kids on a European vacation: “It was wonderful.”

Jennifer Garner is thinking back to a time when she was thriving during her marriage to Ben Affleck.

The 13 Going on 30 alum detailed the unforgettable vacation she took with her and the Gone Girl star’s kids Violet, 20, Fin, 17, and Samuel, 14, before calling it quits with Affleck in 2015.

“When my kids were really little, I went to Paris,” Garner told Ina Garten on a recent episode of her Happy Hour podcast. “Ben was shooting some Batman-ish movie in London and we went for six weeks. I took them out of school. It was the last time I really took kids out of school, but it was wonderful. It was incredible. It was so fun.”

“And part of that is that we bopped around,” Garner recalled. “We went to Venice, we went to Paris.”

And while the Batman v Superman: Dawn of Justice actor was busy getting into character as Bruce Wayne, it was Garten who suggested the Alias actress explore the French city by boat.

Since then, it’s turned into a full-blown tradition for the Daredevil actress. How fabulous is that?

“I’ve done it now, every time I go,” Garner said. “Every picture from that night, from that trip, the smile on my kids’ faces—it was such an adventure that you gave us.”

Garner and Affleck, both 54, tied the knot in 2005 before separating in 2015. And while they finalized their divorce in 2018, they’ve remained a team in co-parenting their little ones.

In fact, The Town actor recently gave his ex her flowers in the parenting department, calling her a “spectacular” mom on the Oct. 11 episode of Sunday Today with Willie Geist before admitting “it always makes me feel like I’m coming up a little short.”

And he’s not afraid to let his kids know that either, sharing he tells them that he’s “doing about 75 percent as good as your mom.”

“And they’re like, ‘Maybe 40 percent,’” he added with a laugh. “I’m maxing out. Woo, B+!”

But in all seriousness, Affleck expressed that he couldn’t be more “grateful” to have a co-parent like the Juno actress.

“I respect her,” he emphasized. “I think the most important thing is having that mutual respect—being able to get along with that person.”

Keep reading for more of Hollywood’s friendliest exes...

No, they are never, ever, ever getting back together. But at this point—between the birthday parties, the friendly embraces and even freaking acting out a sexual fantasy in a star-studded virtual reading of Fast Times at Ridgemont High—it feels like the former Hollywood golden couple is just taunting us.

You know you're cool when you can pop into your ex's talk show and give him a hard time. Such was the case when Silverman turned up on Kimmel's stage in 2018 to chat about her show I Love You, America and his "cute," "bright," "funny" wife Molly McNearney. "It's not fair!" the actress cracked. "She gets, like, new woke Jimmy. I had Man Show Jimmy."

TBH, we might still be mourning this relationship harder than either of its participants. Because when Faris—now married to cinematographer Michael Barrett—heard that Pratt was set to wed Katherine Schwarzenegger in 2019, she offered to officiate. She even sent over a gift after the newlyweds welcomed daughter Lyla in August 2020. Thrilled for son Jack to become a big brother, a source told E! News at the time that Faris "is very supportive."

It would have been super easy for the exes' relationship to become a flop. But more than four years after a messy split, the HGTV stars are still killing it at two projects: parenting kids Taylor and Brayden and turning run-down properties into someone else's dream home.

Literally "best friends" as the rocker put it in a 2013 interview with Oprah Winfrey, the parents to actress Zoe Kravitz still consider themselves kin. "We're one big happy family," Lenny said of his ex, who went on to wed Jason Momoa before their 2020 split. "It's beautiful, and it just shows you what can be done."

After the Dancing With the Stars co-host and former hockey player called in quits in 2020, the pair have continually proved they are on good terms.

In 2025, Hough enjoyed a trip to the lake with Laich and his then-pregnant fiancée Katrín Tanja Davíðsdóttir.

And when Laich announced his marriage to the former Crossfit athlete in 2025, Hough celebrated the news, writing on Instagram, “Congratulations! So so happy for you both. Sending you all the love, health and happiness for the years to come to you and your beautiful family."

Since their 2015 breakup, the parents to Violet, Seraphina and Samuel have hit the conscious uncoupling trifecta: Joint vacations, shared holidays and public displays of admiration with Ben dubbing his ex "an amazing mother" during a 2016 interview with E! News.

Affleck isn't the only one of Garner's exes who she's stayed close with after breakup. She's also friends with her former Alias costar, who she briefly dated in 2003 before her marriage to Affleck.

Chiefs crush Jets 35-12 to end two-game losing streak

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Kenneth Walker had a big hand in the Chiefs’ five-game winning streak to open the season and he had a big hand in ending their two-game losing streak on Sunday.

Walker caught a pair of touchdown passes from Patrick Mahomes in the first half of the game and he ran for another score in the second half of Kansas City’s 35-12 home win over the Jets. The victory moves the Chiefs to 6-2 on the season and Walker is now tied with Jahmyr Gibbs for the league lead with 11 total touchdowns on the season.

It’s the fifth multi-touchdown game of Walker’s first season with the Chiefs and the Chiefs have won all of those games. He did not score in either of their losses, so his importance to the offense probably can’t be overstated.

Walker ran 18 times for 102 yards and his four catches picked up 41 yards, which gave him more yards from scrimmage on Sunday than he managed in either of the Chiefs’ two losses.

The Chiefs got a scare just before halftime when Patrick Mahomes hurt his right elbow on a sack by Jets first-rounder David Bailey. The Chiefs lost a fumble on the play, but Mahomes was able to remain in the game and no negative impact on his game was visible over the second half. Mahomes finished 23-of-31 for 242 yards and four of those completions went to Travis Kelce for 72 yards. Kelce also had a one-yard rushing touchdown.

Mahomes and Kelce have been joined on the top line of Chiefs stars by defensive tackle Chris Jones for many years and Jones chipped in a pair of sacks of Jets quarterback Geno Smith during the win. George Karlaftis had a pair of them as well and the Chiefs will try to keep things firing on all cylinders in Atlanta next Sunday.

One change they could make is with kicker Harrison Butker. He missed another field goal and Justin Tucker is on the practice squad, so the team could opt for a new kicker against the Falcons.

Smith was sacked five times . The Jets actually controlled much of the first half and had chances to put points on the board, but had to settle for five field goal tries. Jason Sanders made four of those kicks, but the Jets needed touchdowns that never came.

They will host the Bills next Sunday and the 2-7 team figures to hear a lot more chatter about Aaron Glenn’s job security in the wake of their fourth straight loss. Five of their first six losses were one-score games, but getting outscored 21-0 in the second half on Sunday doesn’t make much of a case for Glenn having the team on the right path.

‘Avedon’ Review: Ron Howard’s Thrilling Documentary About Richard Avedon, the Great Photographer Who Turned Head-On Portraits Into an Art Form

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Avedon was a paragon of high fashion and also the ultimate democratic humanist. The film explains that paradox and makes his images sing.

“Avedon,” Ron Howard’s documentary about the legendary photographer, is a fantastic movie. It’s the best documentary Ron Howard has made (my favorite before this: “The Beatles: Eight Days a Week”), and it’s been edited in a way that’s so supple and artful that its technique should be studied in filmmaking classes. (The film’s editor is Andrew Morreale.) Woven through the movie are hundreds of photographs that were shot by Richard Avedon, and there’s no question that the images are stunning, but the way the movie layers them into your consciousness is nearly musical in its finesse. And that helps to tell the very large story “Avedon” is telling: how Richard Avedon changed the metaphysics of photography, shifting the way we see the world. Related Stories Paramount Network Orders 13 Original Episodes of Larry the Cable Guy-Hosted Clip Show 'American Mayhem' (EXCLUSIVE)

There’s a paradox running down the center of Avedon’s work that feels as epic and tingly as the 20th century, and that is this: He was a paragon of high fashion — the world of glamour and impossible beauty — but he was also, as a maker of images, the ultimate democratic humanist. The beauty of “Avedon” is that it explains that paradox and makes those images sing. Howard interpolates old clips and interviews, placing Avedon, with his sleek command, at the center of the movie. He also talks to models and critics and media and art-world luminaries who have a great deal of import to say. They include Tina Brown, Larry Gagosian, John Lahr, Twiggy, Adam Gopnik, Hilton Als, Calvin Klein, and Avedon’s lovingly insightful son, John Avedon. James Martin, the executive director of the Richard Avedon Foundation, takes us deep into the labyrinth of Avedon’s archives, where we see the work that resulted from the 16,000 sittings he did from 1944 until his death in 2004. That’s a long time, but it still averages out to one sitting per day, Monday through Friday, for 60 years. The movie also tells the tragic story of his mentally ill sister, whom he revered, and traces his troubled marriages.

“Avedon” opens with an old clip of Richard Avedon, fixing that piercing bird-eye look of his into the camera as he explains, “I can say very little about photography. I follow no rules, no laws, and no advice.” He’s totally serious about that. We then get a montage, set to the funky martial beat of Spoon’s “I Turn My Camera On,” of Avedon portraits of the famous. John Lennon. Orson Welles. Michael Jordan. Elizabeth Taylor. Groucho Marx. Twiggy (in mid-air). Anjelica Huston. Marilyn Monroe lovingly embracing Arthur Miller. Truman Capote. J. Robert Oppenheimer.

All the images look timeless. (Avedon’s lighting makes it look as if each one was taken yesterday.) Every subject looks like what we imagine them looking like, and also different from how you’ve ever seen them. More direct and unfiltered, more intimately themselves. That’s the Avedon touch, but the magic element is that we can’t see how he achieved it. The technique is invisible. There’s a part of one that thinks, “Okay, just set up a camera and put some black-and-white film in it. Place a famous person in front of it. Encourage them to relax and ‘be themselves.’ And voilà! I could be Richard Avedon.”

He probably would have said you’re not wrong.

He wasn’t very interested in the technique of photography. Talk to him about f-stops and he would glaze over. His technique was in the way he approached a session. Iin the film, Isabella Rossellini says that other photographers tend to shoot and shoot and shoot, but Avedon would shoot an image every so often, then tell you to think of something, then shoot again, and end up with a total of maybe 10 shots. He himself explains that if you’ve booked someone for an hour, you don’t have to shoot for an hour. He says that he wastes half an hour having coffee, and then, maybe five minutes before the hour is over, he and the subject will go over to the white paper background, and the magic happens.

The white paper! It has its source in a remarkable story. Avedon was born in New York in 1923, and when he was a kid he says that walking down a street he would blink for a moment and keep his eyes closed, to keep what he saw alive in his head. It was his way of taking pictures before he’d even gotten a camera. He entered the U.S. Merchant Marines when he was 19, securing a place in the photographic department, where he took maybe 100,000 portraits of enlisted men and other workers, all to use for ID cards.

This was the photographer’s equivalent of peeling potatoes on KP duty. But Avedon began to notice something. Shooting these military mug shots against a white background, he encountered “thousands of baffled faces looking into the camera,” and he saw how the photographs revealed these people. That’s one of the startling things about police mug shots. (John Waters: “Everybody looks better under arrest.”) We see a handful of the ID photos that Avedon took, and there it is: the Avedon style. He saw that each face was different, and needed nothing else to be different. So he took what fate handed him — a thankless military assignment — and turned it into a vision. The white background equalized everything. It became his way of making each individual stand out as an individual, with literally nothing to get in the way.

He became a head-on existential classicist, one who echoed Diane Arbus’s eye for highlighting the aspect of a person that was somehow off. The famous Avedon shot of the model Lauren Hutton with her head thrown back and her breast popping out looks as perfectly composed as a Picasso sculpture. But it was totally spontaneous; it just happened. And that’s true of all the famous and iconic gazes Avedon’s portrait subjects gave him. Those looks weren’t planned (how could they have been?).

At the tail-end of a session in 1953, Charlie Chaplin pranked the camera by holding devil horns over his grinning face, and that became one of the most famous images Avedon ever shot. Images like that happened…and then became frozen in the hive mind’s eye. But as “Avedon” explains, part of what he was always recording was the ineffable dance between subject and artist, using each subject to express something about himself. (Chaplin wouldn’t have made those devil horns for anyone else.)

By the time of the 1957 Hollywood musical “Funny Face,” which paired Audrey Hepburn with Fred Astaire as a magazine photographer pointedly based on Avedon, he was already becoming a legend, and he began to use his power as part of his method as an artist. In his 1957 shoot with Marilyn Monroe, pegged to “The Prince and the Showgirl,” the trick was: How could you photograph Marilyn — or, rather, how could you photograph her the way Avedon wanted to photograph her — when she had come prepared, more than anyone else alive, to be photographed? The shoot took an entire day, and it’s full of joyous photographs of Marilyn in full splendor, but the iconic shot that emerged from it was taken at the end of the day, when she let down her guard for 10 seconds, and kind of drooped, and looked a bit sad, and we glimpse, in that photograph, the real Marilyn — not necessarily the forlorn Marilyn, but the full woman behind the image. You see Norma Jean grown up.

“Avedon” is full of great stories and privileged glimpses into how Avedon created some of his most famous images. And, of course, he also photographed the unfamous, like the Oklahoma oil workers he shot in 1980. He used his impish nature to coax fashion models into leaping through the air for his models-in-motion photographs. He waited around for seven weeks in Vietnam to photograph the war’s American generals, and devised singular ways to shoot the Chicago 7 and the denizens of the Warhol Factory and the residents of the new American West, in a series that was so raw and authentic no museum in New York wanted anything to do with it. (He was that far ahead of the curve,) We also hear the story of how he photographed his aging father and used that process to reconcile with him. Avedon supported his own ritzy lifestyle by doing advertising work (including the Obsession perfume TV spots that were like surrealist mini-films), but for him it was never a compromise. And we see how that could be the case. There is, over 60 years, a breathtaking continuity between his portrait work and his fashion work and his commercial work that makes it all inseparable. That’s because every photograph Richard Avedon ever took was striving for the same thing: to open your eyes.

Finally, CBS Confronts Painful Blue Bloods Truth With Boston Spinoff

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Ana Dumaraog is a Writer and Editor at Screen Rant, where she expertly covers Network TV offerings, among other things She has spearheaded the effort to make the site an authority on broadcast shows, initially starting with The Big Bang Theory franchise, NCIS, and One Chicago before taking on the specialization in general. Ana has also covered a wide-spread of topics in the past — from franchises like, the MCU and Star Wars, to classic TV favorites, such as Frasier and MASH.

Ana brings clarity and enthusiasm to every story she tackles—whether reporting on new releases or offering deep dives into fan-favorite franchises. Her coverage translates complex entertainment developments into engaging, audience-focused content.

Warning! Spoilers ahead for Boston Blue season 2, episode 5, "Blood Diamond."Nearly two years after Blue Bloods ended its run, CBS finally confronts the painful truth about it with regard to Boston Blue. For a decade and a half, the Reagan family became a staple of the network's yearly slate, with the Tom Selleck-led series becoming a fan-favorite. Unlike the traditional police procedural, Blue Bloods differentiated itself by making it a family show first and foremost. The project dealt with a group of New York public officials, serving different agencies in the city.

After evading cancellation a couple of times, CBS officially cancelled the series in 2024, with Blue Bloods ending with season 14. The final stretch of the Reagans' run on TV was full of compelling cases and major personal milestones for the clan. It left viewers with the idea that while the series was wrapping, the Reagans' life in New York was going to continue. That was until it was announced that CBS was launching a spinoff with Donny Wahlberg's Danny spearheading Boston Blue. The offshoot has been a success thus far, and both Blue Bloods actors and their characters have been supportive of the expansion project. That said, it has always just been a matter of time before the long-ignored truth about Boston Blue comes to light.

Boston Blue has brought in multiple Reagans throughout the course of its run thus far. That said, its return during the CBS's fall 2026 slate marks the first time that it is featuring Will Estes as Jamie Reagan. The youngest of Frank's kids makes a trip up north to see how his older brother is doing after moving out of New York. Unlike Danny's interactions with Erin, Henry, and even Joe Hill, this latest reunion is tense, as Jamie finally confronts Wahlberg's character about leaving New York, and in effect, him.

Tackling this particularly difficult topic is long-overdue in Boston Blue. Granted that Sean's near-death experience was a justified reason for Danny to follow his son to Beantown, the offshoot arguably glossed over the specifics of his decision to not just stay with Sean temporarily, but uproot his whole life to be up north. For a clan that likes to discuss everything, Jamie reveals in his Boston Blue appearance that Danny barely talked to him about leaving. Blue Bloods was a family show; while the spinoff offered details about their conversations about the move, it didn't offer the whole picture, as it's difficult to imagine that it was an easy transition for the whole Regan clan. It took CBS a while, but thankfully, CBS finally addresses the matter.

It's no secret that Blue Bloods' cancellation was quite controversial. CBS had tried pulling the plug on the series a few times, but the cast and crew rallied to keep the series on the air. Despite the family procedural's popularity during its run, the network's budget cuts motivated them to cancel the series, which was expensive to make, considering that it was filming primarily on location in New York. Still, CBS recognized the popularity of the series and found a way to continue taking advantage of it, which resulted in Boston Blue.

The Raegans are preparing to say goodbye as Blue Bloods heads to its final episodes. Luckily, network TV already has a perfect 100% on Rotten Tomatoes

Considering the core message of Blue Bloods, splitting up the Reagans was the biggest hurdle when justifying the existence of Boston Blue. For over a decade and a half, the family came together every Sunday for family dinner, so seeing Danny leave would have been a major change for the clan. Jamie's debut in Boston Blue offers viewers an idea of how painful that transition was, even as the matter was originally ignored. That said, considering that his move was also for family, it's easier to accept it.

Blue Bloods follows the Reagan family, a multigenerational dynasty dedicated to law enforcement and public service in New York City. As Police Commissioner Frank Reagan and his children serve as a detective, patrol officer, prosecutor, and investigator, each episode balances the demands of policing with the family's personal lives.

Boston Blue follows NYPD officer Danny Reagan as he transitions to a role in the Boston Police Department. Partnered with detective Lena Peters, the eldest daughter of a notable law enforcement family, the series explores their collaboration within the dynamics of Boston's criminal landscape.

The Sci-Fi Masterpiece That Ridley Scott Dismissed as Too “Slow” Is Officially Streaming for Free

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

He has covered everything from Marvel to the Oscars, and Marvel at the Oscars. He also writes obsessively about the box office, charting the many hits and misses that are released weekly, and how their commercial performance shapes public perception. In his time at Collider, he has also helped drive diversity by writing stories about the multiple Indian film industries, with a goal of introducing audiences to a whole new world of cinema.

Director Ridley Scott made a disappointing return to the sci-fi genre this year, with the post-apocalyptic movie The Dog Stars. Starring Jacob Elordi in the lead role, the film grossed around $40 million worldwide against a reported budget of approximately $100 million. Around the same time as the film's release and mediocre reception, Scott announced his intention to return to the Alien franchise, which he helped create in the 1970s. The only trouble is that the franchise was going in an entirely different direction, one that Scott clearly wasn't too impressed by. Scott voiced similar reservations about another reboot of one of his most memorable movies. The movie in question was recently added to a free streaming platform.

A couple of years ago, director Fede Álvarez successfully rebooted the Alien series with Alien: Romulus, which grossed $350 million worldwide against a reported budget of $80 million, and received excellent reviews. A sequel to the movie was quickly announced, but some time later, Álvarez revealed that he wouldn't be returning as director. Scott said in an interview that he found the movie to be just "okay," and that he intends to revisit the narrative that he began with Prometheus and Alien: Covenant.

While Prometheus grossed more than $400 million worldwide, Covenant underperformed with around $240 million globally and brought the franchise to a halt. It wasn't until Romulus that the series appeared to have been resurrected. However, with Scott effectively wanting to erase the movie entirely, it remains to be seen where the series will go next. Curiously, Scott made similar comments about Denis Villeneuve's Blade Runner 2049.

Released in 2017, the legacy sequel received phenomenal reviews but underperformed commercially. It ended up grossing only around $280 million worldwide against a reported budget of more than $180 million. However, many found it to be an improvement over Scott's original, the classic sci-fi noir Blade Runner. Scott disagreed with this contingent, saying in an interview that he found Blade Runner 2049 to be too "slow" and too "long."

The movie now holds a "Certified Fresh" 88% score on the aggregator website Rotten Tomatoes, where the consensus reads, "Visually stunning and narratively satisfying, Blade Runner 2049 deepens and expands its predecessor's story while standing as an impressive filmmaking achievement in its own right." The Blade Runner franchise will be rebooted on November 25 with the Prime Video series Blade Runner 2099, starring Hunter Schafer and Michelle Yeoh. Meanwhile, you can revisit Villeneuve's epic on Pluto TV.
